# Research corpus index

- [Conformance programs scope claims per named profile/capability-set/product-type (not a single global pass/fail), and none of the studied programs require testing the publicly registered production endpoint specifically — applicant-controlled test evidence plus public reproducibility is the norm](pdpp/conformance-claims-are-scoped-per-profile-not-globally-and-no-program-requires-testing-the-registered-production-endpoint.md) — 2026-08-29 — Two-question prior-art sweep for GOVERNANCE.md §4/§4.6 and Core §9. **Q1 scoping**: every program studied (OIDC's OP/RP/FAPI-generation categories, Certified Kubernetes' test-subset-tag + PRODUCT.yaml product-type + versioned-directory submission, ONC's per-`§170.315` criterion CHPL entries, UK Open Banking's separate Functional/Security/DCR tools) scopes a claim along at least two axes — role/direction and profile/tier — never a single global "conforms to the spec" bar; version is a third axis every program treats as load-bearing (Kubernetes ties conformance to an exact minor version; OIDC recertifies per spec revision). Recommends PDPP's §4.6 fix add the version binding GOVERNANCE.md §4.7 "Currency" already half-implies: state which Core §9 role(s)/tier(s) **and against which spec version**. **Q2 integrity/endpoint/fixtures**: confirms via direct primary-source fetch (not just the existing corpus entries) that neither Kubernetes' nor OpenID's submission process requires the tested instance be the applicant's publicly registered production endpoint — both certify from submitted evidence (PR with e2e.log/junit; ZIP of conformance logs) against whatever instance the applicant controls, with integrity coming from post-hoc public reproducibility, not endpoint provenance. **A PDPP rule requiring the registered production endpoint be tested has no precedent in any program studied — it would be a PDPP-original strengthening**, not an industry-standard requirement; if kept, §4.6 (not the suite brief) is the correct home by direct structural analogy to where every other claim-shape rule found (product-type/version binding, per-criterion CHPL scope, per-profile categories) lives — in the certifying body's own process documentation. On data fixtures: only Inferno/ONC visibly solves this, by shipping a "simulated conformant FHIR API" inside the test kit itself rather than mandating vendor-provisioned fixtures or production/PHI testing — the transferable lesson for PDPP is building synthetic-data provisioning into the suite/tooling (spec-core.md's forthcoming §9 suite), matching where Inferno's own solution lives, not into governance.

- [Tim's repeated efficiency instructions to pdpp agents — batch changes, get feedback while it is cheap, delegate down, parallelize without idling, freeze scope, never trade the verification standard for speed — match a single coherent body of prior art (DORA/Accelerate small-batch capability, trunk-based development, test-impact analysis, Google small-CL review culture, Little's Law WIP limits, and LLM cost-cascade routing)](agentic-cli-design/pdpp-working-smarter-playbook.md) — 2026-08-27 — Commissioned by "pretty sure I've already talked to a bunch of you agents about working smaryer like batching changes, getting feedback early when it is cheap etc." Mined 449 raw hits across 357 pdpp session JSONLs plus two full `convo` transcripts for Tim's own verbatim process rules (batch by default but bounded to disjoint/ready work; verify evidence exists before diagnosing and cheap-probe before heavy work; delegate down recursively, escalate only on failure; parallelize but only with zero file contention, never idle mid-window; freeze the acceptance gate instead of chasing an expanding ideal) and grounded each in DORA's small-batch capability (speed and stability are the same lever, not a trade-off), trunk-based development, Google's small-CL review culture, test-impact analysis, preview environments, Little's Law/WIP-limit theory (parallelism needs a disjointness/WIP ceiling or "don't idle" becomes coordination overhead), FrugalGPT-style cost-cascade routing, and incident-response evidence-before-diagnosis practice. The one caveat prior art adds that Tim's own instructions don't state explicitly: Anthropic's own multi-agent research found 15x token cost and reduced effectiveness on tightly-interdependent coding work vs. breadth-first research — parallelize needs a real disjointness check, not just enthusiasm, which is exactly what pdpp's June 2026 249-branch reconciliation incident cost. Cross-checked against two independent audits (STEERING-AUDIT-CODEX.md, GOAL-AUDIT-SINCE-125.md) that found the mechanisms (batching, delegation, parallel lanes, chasing dead time) were followed but agents still substituted reports/passing-isolated-tests/honest-amber-labels for the actual owner-visible, deployed, re-verified outcome — encoded as the checklist's final, hardest rule.

- [Geth v1.17.5 fast-forwards a newly added empty freezer table to the common head but truncates all non-empty tables to the shortest head, so a stale logically non-empty extension table must be reset or rebuilt on a disposable clone before stock cold-open](blockchain-storage/geth-v1-17-5-fast-forwards-new-empty-freezer-tables-but-truncates-to-the-shortest-non-empty-head.md) — 2026-08-23 — Official source-grounded storage behavior behind a Vana archive recovery: `Freezer.repair` excludes `items==0` tables from the minimum-head calculation, advances those tables with `truncateTail(head)`, then truncates every table to the computed common head. `ChainFreezerBALTable` is a separate `bals` freezer added for EIP-7928; pre-Amsterdam ancient writes store nil placeholders. Operational consequence: exact stock Geth on a legacy database whose BALS table has a stale non-zero logical head can truncate current headers/bodies/receipts/hashes to that stale head, while an exact newly empty BALS table can be aligned without discarding legacy tables. Any reset remains a database mutation and belongs only on a disposable clone with byte-level pre/post gates.

- [DECISION for PDPP: bulk ingest freezing the console is caused by unchunked, multi-second single transactions sharing one connection pool with interactive requests in one Node process — Postgres has no query priority and cannot preempt an admitted transaction, so the fix is chunking bulk work into short self-committing transactions, a role-scoped short statement_timeout as backstop, and splitting the shared connection pool, never a read replica or a custom priority scheduler](pdpp/bulk-ingest-must-chunk-into-short-transactions-and-self-pace-because-postgres-has-no-query-priority-and-cannot-preempt-an-admitted-transaction.md) — 2026-08-21 — Live-measured on production (read-only): a 44.5s source-detail page load coincided with an 8.2s `INSERT INTO semantic_search_blob...SELECT` and a 10.7s coverage-scan `SELECT`, both single unchunked transactions; cancelling the ingest dropped the page to 6.0s, proving contention, not just baseline slowness — but EXPLAIN ANALYZE at rest still showed 4014ms for the same SELECT shape (Index Scan on `records_pkey` by `id`, filtering 378,591 rows via no covering index for `(connector_instance_id, stream, deleted, id)`), a genuine second, independent defect. Architecture check: one Node process runs the HTTP server, scheduler, and connector/embedding runtime together, sharing one node-postgres `Pool` with no `max` set (library default 10) — interactive and bulk work draw from the same 10 connections. Confirmed via PostgreSQL's own wiki that Postgres has zero native query-priority/admission-control ("no facilities to limit what resources a particular user, query, or database consumes... set priorities"); `vacuum_cost_delay` is VACUUM/ANALYZE-only, not a general lever; once a transaction is admitted its locks are held for its full duration and nothing external can preempt it (confirmed via lock-mode docs) — so client-side pacing alone (the congestion-control/AIMD corpus entries, correctly identified as solving a *different* problem: outbound pacing against an unknown remote rate limit, not local lock-duration control) is necessary but insufficient without a hard per-transaction duration ceiling. Surveyed Oracle Resource Manager/SQL Server Resource Governor/CockroachDB/TiDB admission control (classify-at-admission + elastic-vs-inelastic lanes is the transferable principle, no mechanism is replicable in Postgres) and Linux CFS SCHED_BATCH/cgroups proportional-share-with-a-floor (avoids priority inversion, unlike naive lower-priority). Recommendation: chunk bulk transactions to a bounded row count/duration (Rails `in_batches` 1000-row precedent), `ALTER ROLE ... SET statement_timeout` as backstop, split the shared Pool into interactive/bulk lanes — all inside the existing process, zero new infrastructure. Explicitly rejects a read replica (Postgres itself is unconstrained; app container is the only capped resource; doubles ops surface for a single-user no-ops-team system) and a custom in-process priority scheduler (can't actually preempt an admitted transaction, so it wouldn't even solve the measured problem). Falsifiable via the same-day canary harness's `sql_scalar`/`must_be_at_most` check kind. Adjacent but distinct from the congestion-control/AIMD corpus (`data-collection-systems/congestion-control-theory-transfers-to-http-scrapers...`, `client-side-rate-governance-uses-three-separable-layers...`) — read those for outbound rate pacing against a remote provider; read this for local DB contention isolation, a different problem with a different (simpler, more bounded) answer.
- [DECISION for PDPP: the runtime cannot decide whether a staged cursor is safe to commit because the protocol types it `cursor: unknown` — `commit_on_success` is a no-information fallback, not a safety judgment; the fix is a self-describing checkpoint carrying a proven-complete boundary, an outstanding-debt list and an identifier-space epoch, which makes safe-at-any-instant true by construction and dissolves the drain question](pdpp/the-checkpoint-protocol-must-carry-a-proven-boundary-and-an-identifier-space-epoch-or-the-runtime-cannot-decide-commit-safety-and-must-fall-back-to-commit-on-success.md) — 2026-08-21 — Answers the ONE question the same-day `interrupted-work-needs-an-owner-fenced-terminal-state` decision doc explicitly gated (its §11: does per-stream commit under INTERRUPTED hold for every connector?). **Root cause is a type, not a policy**: the protocol declares `{type:"STATE"; stream: string; cursor: unknown}` and the runtime's *entire* validation is "non-array object or null" — so `commit_on_success` is the only decision derivable from zero information. The commit MECHANISM is already per-stream and idempotent (`commitState` PUTs one key); only the DECISION is deferred to DONE. **Measured cost, live**: 130,517 `run.state_staged` vs 75,513 `run.state_advanced` — 42% of all staged checkpoint work discarded; 465 terminal runs across 14 connectors durably ingested **897,916 records and advanced no cursor** (chatgpt 362,977 / codex 294,500 / gmail 203,417) — Gmail is 23% of the loss, not the whole of it, so the defect is systemic to the protocol. **Full 43-connector/162-stream survey re-derived from source** (prior "44/45" counts included a fixtures dir): gmail `messages` is confirmed the ONLY `two_pointer` over one ordered space and the only cursor carrying an identifier-space epoch (`uidvalidity`); the unsafe `partition_map_with_global_fallback` exists and is LIVE — slack's `WHERE m.TS > COALESCE(t.last_ts, ?)` with production state showing a 569-channel map alongside a 2026-08-17 global floor, so any channel outside the map has its history below that floor unreachable (plus slack advances the watermark over rows it never emits, per its own in-tree comment). Every other partition map fails SAFE (absent partition ⇒ cold walk). **The decisive negative result**: the dominant fleet shape is the non-positional fingerprint map (cannot skip data either way), and for the positional minority *safety is an accident of emission TIMING, not cursor shape* — heb/groupme newest-first walks are safe only because STATE sits outside the loop; move it inside and you get silent permanent loss with no check firing. **Contract** = 4 fields, each tied to a demonstrated failure: `space` (re-seed detector, IMAP's UIDVALIDITY generalized), `complete_through` (proven-complete boundary), `debt[]` (turns a newest-first walk from footgun to safe), `partition` (makes a global fallback UNREPRESENTABLE rather than discouraged). Rejected: `covered/considered` ratio, a `safe:true` boolean, capability flags (an allowlist with extra steps). **Refutes the parent doc's proposed `covered==considered` gate as a proxy for the wrong property** — coverage honesty and cursor safety merely correlate; github `starred` reports honest `partial` while still skipping data, jellyfin `items` fails the ratio while losing nothing. Runtime decision = 5 connector-agnostic rules; migration falls out automatically (no claim ⇒ stage only, so scaffolds and fingerprint maps never qualify and nobody enumerates them). **Shutdown dissolves**: a checkpoint safe at any instant is safe at the SIGTERM instant by construction — SIGTERM is not a distinguished moment. Only 2 of 17 connectors finish inside Docker's 10s at p95 (slack p95 = 4052.8s = 405× budget; 8 of 17 medians already exceed it), so waiting was never available anyway. Delete the 5s drain; keep successor-adjudication for runs that died between checkpoints; residue is at-least-once redo, never loss.
- [DECISION for PDPP: graceful shutdown is the wrong frame for interrupted work — the invariant is a durable owner-epoch a successor adjudicates, drain is structurally impossible under Docker's 10s default stop, and PDPP's own boot-epoch reconciler is already the right design but is silently disabled in production because `controller_id` defaults to the container hostname](pdpp/interrupted-work-needs-an-owner-fenced-terminal-state-not-a-graceful-shutdown-because-dockers-10s-stop-is-shorter-than-the-work.md) — 2026-08-21 — Decision doc, not a survey. Builds on (and cites rather than restates) the same-day `a-dying-worker-never-writes-its-own-terminal-state` and `when-the-shutdown-grace-window-is-shorter-than-a-unit-of-work` entries, and REFUTES the closing recommendation of 2026-06-14 `bounding-a-hung-job-...-fencing-tokens` for PDPP's case (epoch beats heartbeat here). **Live production measurements are the new ground**: `docker inspect` shows `StopTimeout=<nil>` so prod runs on Docker's 10s default while Gmail runs take minutes; the shipped 5s drain was caught in the logs spending its entire budget and abandoning the run anyway (`drained:0, elapsedMs:5000, timedOut:1`) — a drain is a *negative* win because it burns half the SIGKILL budget doing nothing. **The headline defect**: `PDPP_CONTROLLER_ID` is unset, so `resolveControllerId` falls back to `os.hostname()` = the container ID, fresh every `docker run`; the boot reconciler's ownership filter `COALESCE(controller_id,$2)=$2` therefore excludes every prior container — i.e. every orphan that has ever existed. **121 `run.started` events are permanently non-terminal in prod** (re-measured at 123 forty minutes later — the leak is live, not historical residue), stranded under dozens of dead container IDs back to May, while the older duplicate path writes the wrong vocabulary (134 `run.failed`/`controller_restarted` vs 32 `run.abandoned`), one such run having staged 3 cursors and ingested a batch before being recorded a plain failure. Invariant: *every unit of work carries, in the same durable write that starts it, the identity of the owner epoch that may finish it; a successor adjudicates any unit whose epoch is not its own; interruption is a terminal state written by the successor, never the dying owner.* `INTERRUPTED` must stay distinct from `FAILED` for k8s' reason (`Unknown` ≠ `False` — different remedies; collapsing them wakes the owner at 3am for a deploy). Epoch-derived beats time-derived: no threshold to tune, no false positive — strictly better than every job queue surveyed. **Adjudicates the reaper question**: Oban *shipped* heartbeat rescue, measured 3,600 rows/hr per idle queue, deleted it in v2.0; River closed its heartbeat PR over table bloat; Faktory has heartbeats and still doesn't use them for job recovery — so a heartbeat is a latency optimization, never an architectural escape. Que is the only no-reaper system (PG advisory locks die with the session) and is unavailable to PDPP because runs are `spawn`ed detached children, not holders of the server's DB session. Top-3 deletions: the `controller_restarted` reconciler, the 5s drain, the manual-upload 10-min wall clock. Surfaces (a) runs and (b) device-ingest reservations share one predicate but need two adjudicators; (c) the local outbox is already correct (lease is right there — owner and adjudicator are on different machines) — fit the mechanism to the topology, don't unify for tidiness. Biggest open uncertainty, flagged and gated: per-stream checkpoint commit under `INTERRUPTED` is only as honest as the coverage evidence beneath it, answerable today from the spine by checking whether every `run.state_staged` has a matching `run.detail_coverage_declared` with `covered==considered` and a non-null boundary.

- [When the SIGTERM grace window is shorter than a unit of work, mature systems do not try to finish and do not record Failed — they checkpoint, release the work back to the queue as retryable, and let the next run resume](distributed-systems/when-the-shutdown-grace-window-is-shorter-than-a-unit-of-work-mature-systems-checkpoint-and-requeue-rather-than-finish-or-fail.md) — 2026-08-21 — The SHUTDOWN/DRAIN complement to the 2026-06-14 `bounding-a-hung-job-...-fencing-tokens` entry (detection: heartbeats, wall-clock ceilings, reapers) and to the same-day `a-dying-worker-never-writes-its-own-terminal-state` entry (which establishes that the *service* closes a crashed run); this one answers what the worker should DO and WRITE in the seconds it does get. Grace-window budgets pinned to primary docs: `docker stop` **10s** Linux/30s Windows (daemon default, inherited by `docker run`), k8s `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` **30s**, systemd `DefaultTimeoutStopSec` **90s** (cross-checked against local man pages) — and systemd is the only one with a documented extension protocol (`EXTEND_TIMEOUT_USEC=`). **Trap**: `docker rm -f` and `docker kill` both send SIGKILL and give **zero** drain time, so "which command does the deploy run?" is a correctness question. k8s `preStop` does not add budget, it consumes it and blocks SIGTERM. Three worker strategies, only one scales: *try to finish* (Celery warm shutdown — unbounded by design, hence unusable; Celery 5.5 conceded this with a bounded soft shutdown that still ships **disabled**, `worker_soft_shutdown_timeout` = 0.0); *abandon+requeue whole* (Sidekiq baseline `-t` **25s** then "push those jobs back to Redis"; River `StopAndCancel`); *checkpoint then resume* (Sidekiq Iteration flushes a cursor to Redis and raises `Interrupted` for re-enqueue at the cursor; Temporal heartbeat payload that "the next Activity Task can access and continue with"). The window is a budget for persisting a cursor, **not** for doing work. Terminal-state taxonomy is the corollary: Temporal separates `CANCELED=4`/`TERMINATED=5`/`TIMED_OUT=7` from `FAILED=3` and calls Cancelled "successfully handled a cancellation request"; Cancel permits cleanup, Terminate is invisible to code; failure *types* split by origin (`ApplicationFailure` = user code's own error vs `CanceledFailure`/`TerminatedFailure` = external decision). Kubernetes encodes the identical split as the `DisruptionTarget` pod condition and makes the accounting explicit — `action: Ignore` exists so disruptions don't increment `backoffLimit`. **The Unknown-never-False rule is quotable from k8s API conventions**: "An object that was still active would generally have a `Succeeded` condition with status `Unknown`", write it eagerly, and `Failed=False` "may cause double-negative confusion". Three shipped footguns: Temporal Go `WorkerStopTimeout` defaults to **0s** (in-flight activities neither waited for nor cancelled — safety comes entirely from server-side timeout-then-retry); River treats a cancelled job returning `nil` as **success** so it's never reworked; Celery's default early-ack means a signal-killed task is neither retried nor failed but simply **lost** (`acks_late` alone insufficient — needs `task_reject_on_worker_lost`, default Disabled, warns of message loops). Backstop reapers exist (River rescuer **1h** threshold / 30s sweep; note `JobStuckThreshold` 10s is a *different* in-process mechanism) but River frames the hour of delay as the cost of an unclean exit — reaching the reaper is a shutdown-path bug. Unverified & flagged: Temporal metrics accounting for Canceled/Terminated; any documented link between Celery soft shutdown and Redis visibility timeout (1h default, unconnected page).
- [Lease reclamation (Chubby session leases/jeopardy, etcd lease TTL+keepalive, ZooKeeper ephemeral nodes/herd effect, the precise shape of the Redlock controversy) and checkpoint commit-ordering (Kafka at-most/at-least/exactly-once by commit timing, Flink's Chandy-Lamport barriers, WAL redo/undo) are the two primitives that decide whether interrupted work resumes cheaply or restarts from zero](distributed-systems/lease-reclamation-and-checkpoint-commit-ordering-decide-whether-interrupted-work-can-be-resumed-cheaply-or-must-restart-from-zero.md) — 2026-08-21 — Fills the primary-source lock-SERVICE and checkpoint-COMMIT-ORDERING gap the three same-day sibling entries (`when-the-shutdown-grace-window...`, `a-dying-worker-never-writes...`, `bounding-a-hung-job...`) reference but don't source: Chubby's 12s default lease extension + 45s jeopardy grace period + sequencer-as-fencing-token ancestor; etcd's delete-on-expiry lease primitive powering k8s node Leases and leader election; ZooKeeper's ephemeral-sequential-node recipe as the documented herd-effect fix; and the Redlock disagreement characterized per-axis (clocks, GC pauses, fencing applicability) rather than smoothed over — Kleppmann argues general correctness-under-pause unsafety, antirez's rebuttal is narrower (Redlock's algorithm self-rejects on a pre-check-pause) and reframes Redlock as an efficiency lock, not a correctness lock. Adds Kafka's precise at-most/at-least/exactly-once-by-commit-timing definitions and its "collapse the two commit points" advice for external sinks; Flink's barrier-alignment mechanism traced explicitly to Chandy-Lamport 1985; Postgres/SQLite WAL as REDO vs SQLite rollback-journal as UNDO, both guaranteeing no half-applied crash state; 2PC's textbook "in-doubt transaction" as the strongest classical precedent for a genuinely unknowable outcome; and cursor-pagination/HTTP-Range/tus/rsync as the resumability pattern (durable externally-meaningful checkpoint, not an internal run-tied counter). Application-to-PDPP section (independently derived from reading `runtime/controller.ts`, pre-dating discovery of the same-day `pdpp/interrupted-work-needs-an-owner-fenced-terminal-state...` decision doc) reaches compatible conclusions from the static code alone — flat 5s `CONNECTOR_DRAIN_TIMEOUT_MS`, `controller_restarted` misclassified as `run.failed` — but the `pdpp/` decision doc supersedes it with live production measurements (121+ stranded non-terminal runs, the `PDPP_CONTROLLER_ID`/hostname root cause, a concrete epoch-fenced state machine and migration path) and should be read as the authoritative answer for PDPP specifically; this entry is the generic-prior-art layer underneath it.
- [A dying worker never writes its own terminal state: Temporal's service writes it via timeout expiry (the timeout IS the terminal state) and Kafka's transaction coordinator aborts via transaction.timeout.ms or the next InitProducerId epoch bump — both then downgrade the guarantee to at-least-once execution plus idempotency](distributed-systems/a-dying-worker-never-writes-its-own-terminal-state-the-service-writes-it-via-timeout-and-the-fencing-epoch-invalidates-the-zombie.md) — 2026-08-21 — Primary-source verification (Temporal docs + sdk-go source, KIP-98/KIP-447, Kafka `docs/design/design.md`) of the claim that interrupted work is closed by a durable third party, never by the dying participant. **Confirmed outright**: "The Temporal Server doesn't detect failures when a Worker loses communication with the Server or crashes. Therefore, the Temporal Server relies on the Start-To-Close Timeout to force Activity retries" — there is no crash-detection channel, only timers, so `TimedOut` (written by the service) is the crash state and silence is not a state until a timer converts it. Kafka is structurally identical with two independent reapers, neither being the producer: `transaction.timeout.ms` (coordinator, 60s default) and the successor's `InitProducerId` epoch bump, which both fences the zombie and "Recovers (rolls forward or rolls back) any transaction left incomplete by the previous instance." **Does NOT duplicate** the 2026-06-14 `bounding-a-hung-job-...-fencing-tokens` entry (which surveys heartbeat-vs-wall-clock timeout *selection* and reapers across Sidekiq/k8s/SFN/Celery/GoodJob); this one establishes the stronger structural claim that detection expiry IS the durably-recorded outcome, and adds the whole Kafka EOS half. Sharpest transferable sentences: Temporal guarantees an activity "will be observed as completed exactly once. However, the Activity may be executed multiple times and may even partially complete more than once" — exactly-once is a property of the durable history, not of side effects; Kafka scopes EOS to Kafka-topic-to-Kafka-topic and says "Otherwise, Kafka guarantees at-least-once delivery by default," while calling out overclaimers ("important to read the fine print"). Gotcha for anyone relying on graceful drain: Go SDK `WorkerStopTimeout` **defaults to 0s** and its source comment reads "the time delay before hard terminate worker" — exceeding it just logs "Worker graceful stop timed out." and abandons the activity, which is safe only because the service-side timeout exists. Heartbeat-resume caveat: worker throttles to min(`heartbeatTimeout*0.8` or 30s, 60s) and only "remembers the most recent one," so resume rewinds by up to a throttle interval. Kafka's sleeper lesson for external sinks: collapse the two commit points ("letting the consumer store its offset in the same place as its output") rather than attempting 2PC. Flags doc/KIP disagreement — mainline Kafka docs still advise producer-per-consumer, the exact topology KIP-447 exists to relax.
- [Privy's `ethereum_calldata` policy condition selects the called method with the literal field `function_name` (arguments use `<function>.<input>`), and because the engine defaults to DENY a wrong field string silently bricks the wallet rather than opening a hole](validator-key-custody/privy-calldata-policy-uses-a-function-name-literal-and-fails-closed-so-a-wrong-field-bricks-the-wallet-silently.md) — 2026-08-21 — Supplies the concrete mechanism for the per-transaction value cap that the `vana-escrow-facilitator-role-can-pay-arbitrary-recipients` entry concluded was mandatory. `field: "function_name"` is a magic literal (NOT your function's name, and NOT `method_name`/`method`/`functionName` — none of which appear in Privy's docs); a decoded argument is `<functionName>.<inputName>` e.g. `depositNative.account`, per the docs' own load-bearing comment "'transfer' must match the function name, 'amount' must match an input name." **Inverts the usual misconfiguration intuition**: Privy defaults to DENY when no rule resolves, so a typo'd field fails CLOSED — the wallet signs nothing and the bug looks like an outage, not a breach. Diagnosis is muddied further because simulation runs BEFORE policy evaluation, so reverts/insufficient-funds mask policy errors. The `abi` is mandatory even for zero-arg functions and the method match is NOT implied by a one-function ABI. `function_name` alone permits that selector on ANY contract, so pair it with `to`. **SDK types lie by omission**: `@privy-io/node@0.18.0` types `EthereumCalldataCondition.field` as a bare `string` with no enum while the adjacent `EthereumTypedDataDomainConditionField` IS enumerated — I shipped `method_name` from plausible inference and it typechecked cleanly; only a docs sweep caught it. `EthereumTransactionCondition.field` is a closed union of exactly `to|value|chain_id` and there is no raw-4-byte-selector condition, so ABI decoding is the only route to method-level restriction. At most ONE policy per wallet (`policy_ids` is an array but documented "up to one"), so all constraints share one ruleset. Paired gotcha on the same SDK: `maxRetries` defaults to 2 and retries on pre-response connection errors, so money-moving calls need `maxRetries: 0` + `privy-idempotency-key`.
- [Fine-grained invalidation (Salsa, Bazel/Skyframe, Adapton) invalidates only the derived values that actually read a changed input and re-validates unchanged outputs without re-invalidating downstream — a single coarse generation counter cannot express either distinction](distributed-systems/fine-grained-invalidation-tracks-which-inputs-a-derived-value-actually-read-generation-fences-cannot.md) — 2026-08-21 — Answers the PDPP generation-fence incident (one manifest edit bumped 14 manifests' generation, blanking 15/25 sources to "Not measured" despite intact data) and the "wait for the read model to refresh" false-advice bug. **Does NOT duplicate** the 2026-08-16 code-version-column entry (kept as the coarse "could be stale" fence) or the two 2026-08-19 PDPP condition-taxonomy entries (kept as settled: `ProjectionReliable`'s false-during-run defect, condition renames, passing-row filter) — reads all three and cites them by file:line rather than re-deriving. New ground: Salsa's red-green algorithm + backdating/early-cutoff and Bazel Skyframe's "reverse transitive closure of changed inputs" + change pruning are the same mechanism in two unrelated implementations — record what a derived value actually READ, invalidate only its dependents, and don't propagate invalidation past a re-derived-but-unchanged output. Recommends layering a per-manifest-section dependency map UNDER the existing whole-manifest generation counter (additive, incremental to adopt) rather than replacing it. Names a 4-state taxonomy the existing `unknown` bucket conflates: no-data-ever / unproven-in-flight / **evidence-superseded-by-definition-change** (this ticket) / genuinely-degraded — state 3 needs its own `reason` value (PDPP's condition schema already has the field) because it has a different remedy than state 2, which is why the "wait" copy is actively false for it. Remediation copy rule (NN/g actionability + Plaid's null-when-not-actionable, both already in PDPP's corpus): remediation text must be a pure function of `reason` computed at render time, never a static string hardcoded per condition type — PDPP's `ProjectionReliable` currently hardcodes one remediation for a condition with at least two distinct causes. On auto-trigger: AWS Full/Equal/Decorrelated jitter + Google CRE's self-inflicted-DDoS framing ("15-minute error ⇒ provision 15X capacity") transfer directly to staggering mass-invalidation refresh runs, but interactive-login connectors must be excluded via the manifest's existing `recommended_mode`/`background_safe` flags (new trigger source, same existing flag) — auto-firing an unattended login attempt is worse than the status quo, not better; for those, the fix is the correct `reason`+action copy routed through PDPP's existing attention/required-action mechanism, not an auto-run.
- [npm's trusted-publisher OIDC config requires the package to already exist on the registry — no PyPI-style "pending publisher" for a brand-new name as of mid-2026](npm-registry/npm-trusted-publishing-requires-an-existing-package-no-pending-publisher-like-pypi.md) — 2026-08-21 — Confirms via npm docs + HN (citing PyPI's Trusted Publishing designer) + the community `setup-npm-trusted-publish` placeholder-publish tool: the config UI lives at `npmjs.com/package/${name}/access`, which requires the package to exist. First publish of any brand-new name must be a manual, human-authenticated `npm publish`; trusted publishing only applies from the second publish onward. Practical consequence: an owner click-list for onboarding new packages cannot assume OIDC setup precedes any publish.
- [LFDT's npm continuity mechanism is a shared foundation maintainer account (lfdt-npm) added per-package to existing scopes, not a unified @lfdt npm scope or a documented transfer runbook](lfdt-labs-prior-art/lfdt-npm-is-a-shared-foundation-maintainer-account-added-to-existing-scopes-not-a-single-lfdt-npm-scope.md) — 2026-08-20 — Verified live: `lfdt-npm <npm@lfdecentralizedtrust.org>` co-maintains `@hyperledger/indy-vdr-nodejs` but NOT `@hyperledger/cactus-common` — added per-package, not foundation-wide. No `@lfdt` unifying scope exists. OpenSSF/npm docs endorse OIDC trusted publishing + provenance; OpenJS Foundation explicitly cautions it's "not ready for critical packages" (2FA immaturity, wouldn't stop Shai-Hulud-style attacks). No LFDT-documented npm-transfer/bus-factor runbook found — the shared maintainer account is the de facto (undocumented) mitigation.
- [GroupMe's per-group message count is nested as messages.count on GET /groups, not the flat messages_count the PDPP connector read — which is why 156/156 live groups persisted a null count](pdpp/groupme-per-group-message-count-is-nested-under-messages-not-a-flat-field-2026-08-20.md) — 2026-08-20 — GroupMe's `GET /groups` nests message metadata as `messages: { count, last_message_id, ... }`, while `GET /chats` genuinely does return a flat `messages_count` — a real asymmetry between the two endpoints, not a doc error. The PDPP connector modelled only the flat field for groups, so live data carried `messages_count: null` for 156/156 groups across every version ever collected, while the sibling `members_count` populated normally (values 2–6) — the control that isolates "wrong path" from "sparse data". The committed fixture `__fixtures__/group.json` asserts a synthetic flat `"messages_count": 142`, so fixture and type agreed with each other and disagreed with production; nothing compared either to reality. **Near-miss worth remembering**: binding that field as a completeness anchor would have produced a null-coerced-to-zero denominator for every group — "provider says this group is empty" asserted for groups never fully walked, the same defect class as an `item_count: 0` on unfetched orders. Two transferable rules: check an anchor's live fill rate (`count(*) FILTER (WHERE field IS NOT NULL)`) BEFORE binding it, and never let "provider did not tell us" share a representation with "provider says zero". The `GET /groups/:id/messages` envelope `count` remains genuinely ambiguous in GroupMe's docs (page size vs group total) and must not be bound until confirmed against a real multi-page authenticated response.
- [PDPP's purpose codes, policy vocabulary, and access_mode should each follow IANA-style least-strict registries with dereferenceable pages, DPV/ODRL mapping (not adoption) for policy, and FAPI-grade JAR/mTLS signing materially strengthens non-repudiation over plain bearer auth](pdpp/policy-and-registry-extensibility-prior-art.md) — 2026-08-19 — Three-verdict prior-art sweep for PDPP extensibility decisions. **Q1 purpose-code hosting**: schema.org's per-term dual-serve page (human HTML + machine JSON-LD at the same dereferenceable URI, "Canonical URL: https://schema.org/Person") is the terminal model — closer fit than IANA's one-central-index-page pattern or XML namespaces' deliberately-non-dereferencing identifiers. DPV's canonical-namespace pattern (`w3id.org` permanent redirect + versioned releases + separate discursive "Primer" doc) is worth copying structurally, not its RDF/OWL content model. **Flags a live defect**: spec-core.md's own URIs use `pdpp.org` (Appendix A, `type: "https://pdpp.org/data-access"`) but the deployed docs site and README point to `pdpp.dev` — unresolved canonical-domain split, exactly what IANA Section 2.2 warns causes registry confusion. **Q2 policy vocabulary**: verdict (c) retention-only-with-declared-extension-point, structured so a future `policy.prohibitions[]` field maps 1:1 to ODRL's formal Prohibition/Duty primitives (`odrl:prohibit`, W3C Rec) rather than growing a bespoke enum. P3P's failure (EPIC's "Pretty Poor Policy," ~15% adoption per CyLab) traces to zero legal enforcement and unusable tooling, not vocabulary richness — FAPI/Open Banking prove the same lesson positively: even the highest-assurance OAuth profile in production leaves retention/usage promises contractual, not API-enforced, exactly PDPP's current posture. **Q3a access_mode registry**: RFC 8126 Section 4's ten well-known IANA policies plus its explicit "select the least strict policy" guidance map cleanly onto IANA's live "OAuth Token Type Hints" registry (Specification Required, named designated experts, 2-week SLA, per-entry Change Controller — even a third-party contribution from Kantara's UMA WG) as the precedent to copy verbatim once `access_mode` gets a second value (`event_driven`, already anticipated in spec-deferred.md). **Q3b JAR/FAPI non-repudiation**: RFC 9101 JAR + FAPI Part 2's mandatory JWS-signed, `nbf`/`exp`-bounded (60 min), AS-audience-bound, sender-constrained-token stack materially strengthens the user's technical-legal position over plain RFC 6750 bearer auth — closes the "client can deny requesting X" gap — but proves only signed *intent at request time*, not compliance afterward; retention/no-resale enforcement stays exactly as unprovable as before regardless of signing. PDPP's spec already scopes this correctly as deferred v0.2 hardening (Section 10 DPoP/mTLS note); no v0.1 change needed.
- [FHIR's CapabilityStatement/SearchParameter model (server declares sortable/filterable fields per resource type) is the closest transferable precedent for PDPP's manifest-declared `query.range_filters`/`query.expand`, and UMA 2.0's permission-ticket flow structurally requires pre-registered resources so it cannot express an optimistic ask for a stream that may not exist](pdpp/query-surface-and-discovery-prior-art-2026-08-19.md) — 2026-08-19 — Two-part prior-art sweep for PDPP design notes. **Query surface (Q1)**: FHIR CapabilityStatement declares search params per resource type but leaves `_sort` as SHOULD not MUST and has no standard way to declare mandatory parameter combinations (real servers diverge — Azure single-field sort only, Smile CDR chained+indexed); UK Open Banking's Transactions endpoint is the strongest "stay minimal" precedent (one regulator-mandated date-window filter, no general sort/filter grammar, explicit graceful-degradation rules at range edges); OData/$filter/$orderby is the maximal-power contrast with documented adoption costs (SQL-translation risk, N+1 via $expand, mandatory allow-listing as a security boundary); current MCP server-design guidance converges on small default page sizes + cursor pagination + narrow composable tools, which favors PDPP's existing minimal base surface over OData-style richness. Recommends the "declared-capability" option (extend the `query` block PDPP already has to add declared sortable fields) over "stay minimal forever" or "adopt OData-shaped filters." **Discovery/absence-leak (Q2)**: UMA 2.0 cannot express the owner's optimistic-ask flow at all — resources must be pre-registered before any permission-ticket flow, so an unregistered resource is a protocol ERROR, not a routable outcome; OIDC's essential-claims parameter is the closest existing "ask for something that might not exist" mechanism but Core spec leaves omitted-claim behavior undefined (the ASC extension had to be drafted to fix this); OAuth incremental/step-up authorization is documented as actively leaky (accumulated-scope disclosure, step-up challenge params correlating with high-value-resource holders); GNAP's continuation flow (RFC 9635) is the best structural fit — AS can respond to an unreachable/denying resource owner and let the client "negotiate next steps" without the initial ask needing pre-registration — and matches spec-core.md's own stated posture that GNAP should be evaluated as a future authorization-server foundation. Includes an absence-leak comparison table across all five prior-arts and three ranked candidate shapes for PDPP (GNAP-continuation-flavored optimistic ask ranked best fit).
- [OIDC/UK-OpenBanking/FHIR/JSON:API converge on optional-absent (not required-nullable) for "no expiry"; pagination limits are server-advertised not spec-fixed; RFC/FHIR prose does not caption JSON examples as numbered figures](api-contract-design/no-expiry-is-optional-absent-not-required-nullable-and-spec-prose-doesnt-caption-json-examples.md) — 2026-08-19 — researched for PDPP spec-core.md editorial decisions. UK Open Banking `ExpirationDateTime` on `account-access-consents` is `0..1` optional with spec text "If this is not populated, the permissions will be open ended" — the closest analog to PDPP's grant, and it lands on optional-absent not required+nullable. OIDC's `exp` is REQUIRED only because OIDC tokens always expire (no "no-expiry" state exists there at all — different domain shape, not a counter-example). FHIR primitive "no value" = omitted element, never a JSON `null`. FHIR `_count` paging is entirely server-discretionary, no spec-fixed default/max anywhere in core FHIR — PDPP's fixed 25/100 is more prescriptive than the FHIR norm (legitimate tradeoff, not free). RFC 7322 has no numbered-figure/caption requirement for JSON examples; OIDC Core itself uses a bare prose lead-in ("non-normative example") matching PDPP's "Example:" convention. SMART on FHIR's scope tables carry no "which base spec" provenance column — distinction lives in prose, weak evidence PDPP's Status column (semantic class, not RFC-of-origin) already exceeds typical practice.


- [Verifying a local copy against an upstream you don't control has only FOUR one-sided techniques (internal consistency, watermark regression, sequence-gap detection, source-frame sampling) — every Merkle/set-reconciliation scheme structurally requires the source to hash under a partitioning and canonicalization the CLIENT chose, and the backup industry's flagship checks (`restic check`, `borg check`, `zpool scrub`) verify a repository against ITSELF and would pass on a copy that silently missed half the source](data-collection-systems/verifying-a-local-copy-against-an-uncooperative-upstream-has-only-four-one-sided-techniques-because-every-merkle-scheme-requires-the-source-to-hash-on-your-terms.md) — 2026-08-19 — **The MECHANISM layer (what can you actually know, and what does it cost); the health-vocabulary and permanently-partial-copy entries settle only how to PRESENT it.** restic concedes the crux in vendor words: "**Restic cannot detect if data read from stdin is complete or not**" — its worked example is a failed `mysqldump` producing an *empty* backup that every integrity check passes, with the remedy pushed explicitly "outside of restic". Only `rclone check` has an external referent, emitting `+` (missing on destination) — the verdict self-checks structurally cannot produce — but its cheap path needs "a common hash type" or it falls back to `--download` of both sides. AWS Backup restore testing proves **restorability, not fidelity** (content validation is a customer-written Lambda stub). The negative result is structural, not a search gap: Cassandra/Dynamo/rsync/Syncthing/IBF/range-reconciliation all require a THREE-part conjunction — the source runs your code, agrees on your partitioning, agrees on your canonicalization — so no consumer API can ever participate. What providers *volunteer* is the whole game: IMAP is the one graduated ladder (`UIDVALIDITY` free → `STATUS MESSAGES` exact in 1 round trip → `HIGHESTMODSEQ` → QRESYNC `VANISHED`, each degrading safely); RFC 6578 defines snapshot-vs-delta NORMATIVELY (empty sync-token "**MUST return all member URLs**", non-empty returns changes only) — the exact Apple-Contacts-style bug, pre-answered by the RFC. Gmail ships an *exact* `messagesTotal` AND an *estimated* `resultSizeEstimate` (never conflate); `historyId` and IMAP UIDs are both explicitly NON-contiguous so gap detection is unavailable where you'd most expect it; Slack has no total-count field at all. Sampling math is primary-sourced (Hanley & Lippman-Hand, JAMA 1983) and recomputed locally: n=300 all-present ⇒ ≤1% miss at 95% (exact 0.9936%). Its fatal misuse is named by PCAOB AS 2315: "confirming recorded receivables cannot be relied on to reveal unrecorded receivables" — **sampling from the copy you hold proves nothing at any n**; the frame must be source-side. Prefer restic's deterministic `n/t` rotation over random draws (the percentage form "will not guarantee to cover all available pack files after sufficient runs"). Ranked 10-technique table (catches/misses/cost/upstream requirement/who ships it), a 4-tier structure, and a proposed per-stream `verification` manifest block (`total`/`boundary`/`sequence`/`delta`/`inventory_call`/`sampling`) orthogonal to PDPP's existing `coverage_strategy` — which describes how a run WALKS a source, never how a claim is CHECKED, hence `covered == considered` holding by construction. Honest gaps: no consumer personal-data product verifies completeness upstream (white space); no numeric cadence guidance exists anywhere (restic ships the mechanism and declines to recommend an interval); "an untested backup is not a backup" has NO primary source, not even CISA's.
- [Consumer sync products almost never model "working AND permanently partially incomplete" — Nextcloud's `SyncResult::Problem` ("Sync was successful but some files were ignored") is the only shipped success-with-a-permanent-gap tier; finished one-time imports are an EVENT not a STATE; and no product ships a fused "last updated X · syncing now" string](ux-writing/permanently-partial-sync-has-almost-no-consumer-prior-art-and-nextclouds-sync-was-successful-but-some-files-were-ignored-is-the-only-shipped-success-with-a-permanent-gap-tier.md) — 2026-08-19 — **The exact-copy/vocabulary layer under the three product-design + api-contract-design status entries below; those settle whether to show a list, this settles what WORDS go on it.** Written for the corrected premise that PDPP is a CONSUMER product. Answers three states no prior survey covered. **(1) Permanently partial (Gmail: 200k records fine, 36 attachments over a 25 MB cap that can NEVER be collected).** Nextcloud desktop is the ONLY shipped example of partiality as a *peer* of success: `Theme::statusHeaderText` switches `SyncResult::Problem` → **"Sync was successful but some files were ignored"**, a distinct enum member beside `Success` ("Sync was successful") and `Error` ("Error occurred during sync") — verified in raw `src/libsync/theme.cpp`. Size caps are stated as plain fact with items listed inline and NO error framing: **"There are folders that were not synchronized because they are too big: "** + the folder list, rendered into `_ui->selectiveSyncNotification` — a **persistent Account Settings notification, not a one-time consent modal** (this read *corrects* a delegated agent's opposite claim). Most transferable rule: Nextcloud splits permanent from retryable **by grammatical tense** — permanent = future declarative ("It will not be synchronized"), retryable = past + log pointer ("could not be synced due to an error. See the log for details"). Its trap: `Problem` and `Error` copy differ only by a contraction ("Some files could not be synced!" vs "Some files couldn't be synced!") + warnings-vs-errors + icon, and a community thread shows the ignored-item LIST is unreachable. Syncthing supplies the missing half — the count IS the link ("Show the number of failed items as a link to bring up the list") and **"Reduced by ignore patterns"** annotates a deliberately-smaller count in place — but its failed-items list is explicitly retryable ("They are retried automatically and will be synced when the error is resolved."), so it is the wrong model to copy. Everywhere else collapses permanent into retryable: Dropbox one tooltip ("Your file can't update or sync"), OneDrive puts "This file is too big" on a different page from its can't-sync article with no distinct client treatment, Google Drive dumps non-bandwidth failures into a "Lost and Found" folder. **(2) Finished one-time imports (Google Maps 299,248 / WhatsApp 120,042 records, zero runs ever).** Cleanest negative: across Bitwarden ("Data successfully imported"), PhotoPrism ("Import completed in %d s"), Obsidian ("Import complete." + a deletable log NOTE), import completion is a **transient toast** and the records merge indistinguishably — no persistent import badge anywhere. Only real precedent is Firefly III *declining to invent vocabulary*: an account list holding both CSV-loaded and bridge-fed accounts uses generic `'Active'`/`'Inactive'` + a column headed **"Last activity"**, never "Last synced". Takeout dodges structurally (exports expire ~7d). **1Password's "Imported from LastPass" is a BREACH-warning category, not an import status** — the brief's strongest lead, retired by the primary source. **(3) Long routine mid-sync (YNAB: hourly, 20 min each).** **No product fuses last-known-good with in-flight in one sentence** — the three shipped approaches are two separate labeled fields (Syncthing state + "Last Scan"), sequential state replacement (Nextcloud actively BLANKS its detail line via `setSyncStatusDetailString("")`), or an icon swap. And icon-only has a documented cost: nextcloud/desktop#2402 "Status icon in taskbar shows client fully synced while sync is actually running." So PDPP's existing enforced "Refreshing now." annotation is AHEAD of the surveyed field. Progress format to borrow: "Syncing %1 of %2  (%3 left)". Nobody ships stuck/timeout copy at all. **Proposed verbatim vocabulary**: `Healthy` + "36 attachments are too large to collect. They will not be collected." + link "See the 36 items" → "Items that will not be collected" / "Larger than the 25 MB limit (48.2 MB)"; `Imported` + "Imported 12 March 2026 · 299,248 records" (never "Last synced"/"Never run"/"Stale"); "Updated 47 minutes ago" + "Refreshing now · 1,204 of 8,000". **Where consumer framing FAILS (no honest rendering exists)**: "Connector code needs a fix" (owner isn't the actor → per Plaid, emit NOTHING), "Not measured"/`ProjectionReliable` (make it unreachable, don't reword it), and the raw 13-row list (filter, don't reword). Biggest disclosed gap: Monarch Money's "Manual Accounts" — the best-named candidate for a one-time category beside live syncs — 403'd on every fetch, exact copy UNVERIFIED (~40%); attack that first.
- [RED TEAM: consumer products DO show raw condition lists to non-developer owners, PDPP's conditions are a clock-and-sweep-driven materialized view (not a fetch) so the two-axis redesign misfits, and the grant-scoped maintainer-text leak does not exist in production code](product-design/consumer-products-do-show-raw-condition-lists-to-owners-and-pdpp-is-a-materialized-view-not-a-fetch-so-the-two-axis-redesign-is-refuted-in-favor-of-a-minimal-fix.md) — 2026-08-19 — **Adversarial entry attacking the two same-day entries below; both are corrected in place. Read this before acting on either.** THREE REFUTATIONS. (1) **"No consumer product shows a raw condition list to a non-developer owner" is FALSE.** 1Password Watchtower's dashboard IS the list ("Any Watchtower category that has items in it will appear on your dashboard") for a consumer audience; Nextcloud ships **71** named `SetupCheck` classes with a documented four-state per-check vocabulary (success / info = "cannot be guaranteed that the check passed" / warning / error) on the standard admin "Security & setup warnings" panel — its `info` tier is exactly the `unknown` state the original entry assumed nobody shows an owner; Bitwarden ships six named reports with no aggregate score; and **Home Assistant, the cited source of the rule, tells owners to "see the list of issues that need your attention."** The surviving narrower rule is a FILTER not a prohibition: nobody renders the *passing* rows. (2) **The TanStack/GitHub two-axis analogy misfits PDPP, which already implements the split.** `connection-health.ts:22-25` shipped it as a named decision ("Ordered Projection Plus Orthogonal Axes"): "`syncing` and `stale` are NOT headline states... exposed as orthogonal axes/badges." And PDPP is a materialized view, not a fetch — only 1 of 13 conditions is settled by a run finishing; `Fresh` flips on `Date.now()` with no run (`freshness.ts:44,59-61`), `RetryPolicyClear`/`AttentionClear` expire on the clock, `ProjectionReliable` is sweep-repaired, and each condition carries its OWN `observed_at`, so there is no single as-of moment to settle to. `verdict = null until settled` is vacuous for import-only connectors that will never run again. Cost of the redesign: **265 label literals across 31 files** + rework of an 11-invariant gate whose inv-6 recomputes `pill.label`. (3) **The grant-scoped leak is not live**: `toGrantScopedVerdict` has ZERO production callers ("Dispatch C wires this at the wire seam" — never wired); both `rendered_verdict` emitters are `requireOwner`-gated; an MCP test asserts it "must not appear in MCP grant-scoped reads"; the public OpenAPI has no health field. SURVIVED genuine attack: the `ProjectionReliable` binary-false-while-running defect (tell: its own remediation is already `action: "wait"`), and last-known-good discipline — PDPP *exceeds* the RFC 5861 bar via an enforced invariant ("off-fresh verdict is missing its co-required freshness annotation (inv 1)") and already labels in-flight staleness "Refreshing now."; only residual is an unbounded annotation on a hung run. **VERDICT: minimal fix (give `ProjectionReliable` an `unknown` branch; filter passing conditions from the owner surface; bound the stale annotation), NOT the two-axis redesign.** ~~Persona finding: PDPP's spec calls the surface the "operator console" the *owner* drives and the quickstart calls it "owner-mode administration" — owner and operator are one technical person, so the consumer prior art was imported for a persona this product does not have.~~ ⚠️ **PERSONA FINDING VOID (2026-08-19): the product owner states PDPP is a CONSUMER product.** The cited evidence was a directory NAME (`docs/operator/`), a directory description, and one sentence about who starts a run; a re-check found **no written audience statement in the repo at all** (README, spec-core, spec-architecture all checked), and the spec's only "consumer" refers to a *data source*. Corrected in place. **The condition-list conclusion SURVIVES on the other leg** — Watchtower/Bitwarden/HA are consumer products showing lists to consumers, so that precedent gets *stronger*, not weaker. What flips: the passing-row FILTER is promoted from good practice to the load-bearing requirement, and the condition RENAMES in the companion `api-contract-design` entry (`ProjectionReliable` → `DataQueryable`, etc.) move from taxonomy hygiene to a product requirement, since owner-visible rows now face a consumer. Unchanged: all PDPP code findings, the materialized-view and grant-leak refutations, and the 265-literal cost census.
- [Data-possession and work-in-flight are orthogonal status axes, mixed-polarity condition lists are provably not summarizable, and the owner/maintainer message split is by ACTOR not by tone — the entry's "never shown to a non-developer owner" claim was REFUTED 2026-08-19](product-design/data-possession-and-work-in-flight-are-orthogonal-axes-and-the-owner-maintainer-message-split-is-by-actor-not-tone.md) — ⚠️ **PARTIALLY REFUTED 2026-08-19 — the "no consumer product shows a raw condition list" claim is FALSE; corrected in place. Read the red-team entry above first.** — 2026-08-19 — The product/UX layer above the same-day `api-contract-design/health-vocabularies-...` entry (which owns the k8s condition-SCHEMA layer); read that one first. THREE load-bearing findings. (1) **Two axes, not one**: TanStack Query splits `status` ("do we have data?") from `fetchStatus` ("is the queryFn running?") under an explicit "Why two different states?" heading, because "Background refetches and stale-while-revalidate logic make all combinations possible"; SWR shipped the collapsed version FIRST and added `isLoading` in v2 after `isValidating` proved ambiguous — two independent libs, one converged answer, and a refresh is exactly the case that forces the split. Errors split the same way (`isLoadingError` vs `isRefetchError`). (2) **Last-known-good is legitimate but must be LABELED and BOUNDED**: RFC 5861 mandates a non-zero Age + Warning header and an expiry ("SHOULD NOT continue to be served stale"); TanStack's `placeholderData` forces `success` status but sets `isPlaceholderData` and gates interaction; Prometheus writes an explicit stale MARKER (series vanish, never flatline; the 5m lookback is only the post-restart fallback); Grafana separates FOUR concepts — Normal/NoData/Error/Stale — and warns against relying on Keep-Last-State; Datadog picks per data type (missing count = real 0, missing gauge = last known). Nagios `UNKNOWN` = the CHECKER failed, not staleness. (3) **NEGATIVE evidence on condition lists** — ⚠️ **REFUTED 2026-08-19, see the red-team entry above; the surviving rule is only that nobody renders the PASSING rows**. The refuted original read "no surveyed consumer/prosumer product renders named booleans as its primary surface"; 1Password Watchtower, Nextcloud's 71 setup checks, Bitwarden's six reports, and Home Assistant's own "list of issues that need your attention" are all counterexamples. What still stands from this survey: Home Assistant Repairs gates owner-facing issues on actionability with "There are no exceptions to this rule" (non-actionable detail → logs, once, + redacted diagnostics blob; schema `data` is "Arbitrary data, not shown to the user"), HA splits `unavailable` (no data fetched) from `unknown` (fetched but incomplete), ZFS/Synology collapse to 3 states and drill down only on the bad ones, and even kubectl shows a SYNTHESIZED display string. Plus: **owner/maintainer split is by ACTOR** — Plaid's `display_message` is "`null` if the error is not related to user action" and ships null even for ITEM_LOGIN_REQUIRED, so the right question is "is the owner the actor?" not "how do I phrase this gently"; Stripe adds a third bucket (text deliberately DEGRADED because accuracy harms: `fraudulent`/`stolen_card` → present as `generic_decline`) and ships two literal audience columns. Enforcement is only type-level (AIP-193's separate `LocalizedMessage`; NSError's `NSDebugDescriptionErrorKey` "will never be used in localizedDescription") or lint-level (`eslint-plugin-i18next` no-literal-string); GOV.UK bans jargon by example but mandates no review. Wait-states assert no-action AND are structurally checkable (Stripe `pending_verification` says "No action is required" while `currently_due` is empty) with a bounded range + cause of variance + typical case + promise to notify (Takeout). Incompleteness is a NAMED enum, not an error (Plaid `NOT_READY`/`INITIAL_UPDATE_COMPLETE`/`HISTORICAL_UPDATE_COMPLETE`). Unverified: UniFi, Takeout live screen, Grafana panel-refresh retention, Polaris, Salesforce.
- [Production health vocabularies separate a lifecycle axis from a verdict axis, and Kubernetes explicitly REFUSES to recommend positive condition polarity — the widely-quoted "abnormal-true" rule was removed from api-conventions.md](api-contract-design/health-vocabularies-separate-a-lifecycle-axis-from-a-verdict-axis-and-kubernetes-explicitly-refuses-to-recommend-positive-condition-polarity.md) — 2026-08-19 — ⚠️ **The PRIOR-ART half (Kubernetes/GitHub/systemd/ELB/Plaid source readings) re-verified clean and stands. The APPLIED half is partially refuted (2026-08-19): PDPP already implements the axis split, the two-axis refactor is not worth 265 label literals, and the grant-scoped leak has no production caller — both corrected in place. Read the red-team entry at the top of this index first.** — The naming/taxonomy layer beneath integration-health UX (companion to `feedback-systems/integration-health-ui-converges-...`, which covers the product-survey layer). Verified by reading raw api-conventions.md master: "neither positive nor negative polarity can be recommended as a general rule" — `abnormal-true` appears nowhere in the current doc and survives only in third-party explainers, so "k8s recommends positive polarity" is FALSE. K8s does give the load-bearing rule for in-flight state: intermediate states set `status: Unknown`, never `False` ("An object that was still active would generally have a `Succeeded` condition with status `Unknown`"), plus absent≡Unknown, additive/open-world conditions, adjective-or-past-participle naming, required CamelCase `reason` vs human `message` split by render surface, and the reason `phase` was deprecated (enum extensibility, not that lifecycle is wrong — Pod still ships BOTH `phase` and `conditions`). Two-axis (GitHub check-runs `status`×`conclusion`, where conclusion is legitimately `null` until completed; systemd LOAD/ACTIVE/SUB where "ACTIVE = generalization of SUB") beats one-axis (AWS ELB, Consul) — ELB had to spend three enum slots (`initial`/`unused`/`unavailable`) on non-failures to stop them reading as `unhealthy`, and GitHub's skipped-workflow-stuck-Pending footgun is the documented proof that an unrepresentable lifecycle reads as failure. `unknown` vs `not_applicable` rendering rule: not_applicable is the ABSENCE of a condition (drop it from the list entirely), `unknown` is written-but-neutral and can only withhold `healthy`, never produce `degraded`. Enforceable reason→message boundary from Plaid: `display_message` is "`null` if the error is not related to user action" — make audience structural (discriminated union, no `cta` field on maintainer actions) rather than a convention. Also: ELB namespaces reason codes by blame (`Elb.*` vs `Target.*`); Prometheus separates measured-false (`up==0`) from never-measured (absent series) structurally. Applied verdict on PDPP's 13 conditions with file:line evidence (13→10, 2 owner-hidden): `ProjectionReliable` is `false` during in-flight runs via a deferred-evidence chain and races a SECOND liveness route (`badges.syncing`), so a running connection can render "Not measured"; `BacklogClear` and `LocalExporterAvailable` read the same `outbox` axis to OPPOSITE polarity; `GrantScopedVerdict` strips only `detail`/`trace`, not `required_actions` — ⚠️ but the claim that "Connector code needs a fix" therefore **reaches owners** was REFUTED 2026-08-19: `toGrantScopedVerdict` has zero production callers, both `rendered_verdict` emitters are `requireOwner`-gated, and no grant-scoped surface serves the field. Latent type-level gap, not a live leak.

- [Path-filtering a required GitHub Actions workflow deadlocks unrelated PRs, but job-level if: skips report Success and do not](github-actions/path-filtered-required-workflows-deadlock-prs-but-job-level-if-skips-report-success.md) — 2026-08-18 — A required workflow with a `pull_request.paths` filter leaves its check permanently "Pending" (not Success) for PRs that don't touch those paths, blocking merge forever; GitHub's docs prescribe removing the trigger-level filter and computing relevance inside the workflow instead. But a job SKIPPED via job-level `if:` reports Success and does not block merging — and GitHub Actions implicitly ANDs `success()` into any job-level `if:` that doesn't already call a status function (confirmed via github/docs#4823), so a real failure in the relevance-computing job itself makes every downstream job skip-as-Success, silently passing a required-check surface with nothing having run. Correct pattern: no trigger filter + an always-run `changes` job + `needs`/`if`-gated expensive jobs + one additional always-run (`if: always()`) aggregate gate job that explicitly inspects every `needs.<job>.result` (including the `changes` job's own) and is the ONLY job name listed in branch protection — never the individual gated leaf names.

- [Central git-hook policy is distributed by runtime reference plus an immutable per-repo pin — the pin is the only thing that must be duplicated, and Renovate customManagers can bump it across repos](repo-hygiene-and-secret-scanning/central-hook-policy-is-distributed-by-runtime-reference-plus-an-immutable-per-repo-pin-and-only-the-pin-is-duplicated.md) — 2026-08-18 — pre-commit is the canonical inversion-of-control case: hook logic stays central, is cloned into a machine-local cache, and each repo carries only a stub plus a `rev:` pin — and pre-commit explicitly does NOT support branch names ("not supported... will NOT update automatically"), so the per-repo pin is the design, not a wart. Scorecard names the cost of pinning ("can inhibit software updates") and prescribes automation, not mutability, as the fix; tj-actions/CVE-2025-30066 supports this empirically (hash-pinned consumers "would not be impacted"). The complication: SHA pinning gives content immutability but NOT provenance — GitHub does not validate that a SHA belongs to the named repo (forks share an object graph), so origin must be checked separately. `.github` itself is a read-time fallback + reference library, NOT a control plane: health files never enter clones, reusable workflows are runtime `uses:` references, org "required workflows" was deprecated into rulesets, and nothing pushes files into member repos without a bot. Practical upshot: shrink the per-repo footprint to the pin alone and let Renovate `customManagers` (file-type agnostic, `currentDigest` capture group) bump a `CENTRAL_POLICY_SHA='<40-hex>'` in a bash script — untested for SHA-format handling, needs a trial. Client-side hooks stay bypassable (`--no-verify`), so CI must mirror any check that matters.

- [GitHub token scopes and authentication for multi-repo sync bots: three token types (fine-grained PAT for 5–50 repos, GitHub App for org-wide auto-scaling, GITHUB_TOKEN for single-repo Actions); minimum scopes for read-N-repos (Contents:read), write-N-repos (Contents:write), open-PRs (Contents + PRs + Metadata), and workflow updates (Contents + Workflows); a single org-scoped token covers multiple repos via PAT repository-selection or App org-wide access, but GitHub App recommended for ≥50 repos due to auto-rotation (1 hour vs 1 year), no manual repo-list maintenance, and rate-limit scaling (5–12.5k/hr); rate limits: PAT/App 5k/hr (standard), GITHUB_TOKEN 1k/hr per repo; real implementations reviewed: Renovate (GitHub App preferred, Contents + PRs + Workflows), Dependabot (built-in, no external token), repo-file-sync-action (requires GIT_INSTALLATION_TOKEN or PAT with repo scope, cannot use GITHUB_TOKEN for multi-repo), actions/checkout (default contents:read, needs custom PAT for private external repos); security best practice: never use classic PAT (deprecated), prefer fine-grained PAT + explicit repo selection or GitHub App with org approval](github-token-scopes-multi-repo-bots.md) — 2026-08-18 — Comprehensive decision tree and permission reference for token authentication in multi-repository workflows. Three primary token types: Personal Access Tokens (fine-grained — recommended for ≤50 repos), GitHub Apps (recommended for ≥50 repos and org-wide deployment), GITHUB_TOKEN (single-repo Actions default). Multi-repo mechanics differ by type: fine-grained PAT requires explicit repository selection (no auto-expansion to new repos), classic PAT grants all-repos access (too broad, deprecated), GitHub App auto-grants all org repos unless org admin restricts. Key finding on repository access: scopes are a restriction layer *over* repository access, not a permission grant; a token cannot access a repo the owner isn't already a collaborator on. Minimum scope table provided for four common operations: (a) read N repos = Contents:read, (b) write/push N repos = Contents:write + Metadata:read, (c) open PRs = Contents + PRs + Metadata permissions, (d) update workflows = Contents + Workflows permissions. Org-scoped token across multiple repos is possible with fine-grained PAT (explicit repo list, single org) but scales poorly beyond 50 repos; GitHub App is the standard pattern for scaling (org install, automatic scope expansion, shorter-lived tokens, better audit). Rate limit comparison: classic/fine-grained PAT 5,000 req/hr (15,000 Enterprise), GitHub App 5,000–12,500 req/hr (scales with org size), GITHUB_TOKEN 1,000 req/hr per repo. Real-world testing against Renovate, Dependabot, repo-file-sync-action, and actions/checkout verified actual authentication choices. Security guidance: reject classic PAT, use fine-grained PAT for personal scripts (scope to your repos, 30–90 day expiration), deploy GitHub App for org bots (org approval, 1-hour auto-rotation, audit trail). Tables included: tradeoff matrix (PAT vs App), scope listing (OAuth and fine-grained), minimum scopes per operation, use-case recommendations. Common gotchas: GITHUB_TOKEN won't work cross-repo, scopes don't grant extra permissions, fine-grained PAT doesn't auto-expand to new repos, token expiration requires renewal (PAT) or is automatic (App).

- [Sync bots must choose between push (direct commit, low friction, requires branch-protection bypass) and PR (audit trail, native protection, one CI cycle slower); GitHub Apps are the secure foundation for cross-org access (org-scoped tokens, auto-rotation) versus PATs; auto-merge with CI signals enables both velocity and full audit trail; Renovate defaults to PR with auto-merge, repo-file-sync-action defaults to PR with optional push, and CNCF Clowarden demonstrates multi-org app deployment](sync-bots-push-vs-pr-tradeoffs-and-cross-org-permissions.md) — 2026-08-18 — Decision framework spans three axes: (1) PUSH vs PR: push is fastest for trusted generated content but requires explicit bypass ruleset, PR is auditable and native to branch protection; (2) AUTO-MERGE: GitHub's native feature + CI pass signals achieve velocity without sacrificing audit; (3) CROSS-ORG: GitHub Apps (org-scoped tokens, 60-min auto-rotation, audit trail) beat PATs and personal tokens; a single app installs in multiple orgs but auth is per-org per request. Tested against real implementations: Renovate's hybrid (PR default + auto-merge, push for low-risk, major-version PRs unchecked), repo-file-sync-action (PR default + `SKIP_PR` flag), Dependabot (always PR), CNCF Clowarden (multi-org webhook demuxing). Key tradeoff table: push wins on velocity, PR wins on compliance/audit/permission-isolation. Recommendation: PR + auto-merge (default) for compliance-sensitive work, push only after 3+ months error-free; GitHub App + fine-grained PAT (enterprise-scoped) for multi-org; reject classic PATs and user tokens. Covers: branch protection bypass scope, bot credential rotation, approval gates, cross-org permission models, auto-merge policy conditions, GITHUB_TOKEN limitations, and audit trail requirements.


- [download-artifact with `pattern` and default `merge-multiple: false` nests each artifact in a subdirectory named after the artifact, a skipped/failed job's `needs.*.outputs` is an empty object (so outputs read as empty string), and buildx `imagetools` needs no builder while `--format '{{json .Manifest.Digest}}'` emits a quoted digest with NO trailing newline](github-actions/download-artifact-pattern-without-merge-multiple-nests-each-artifact-in-its-own-subdirectory-and-skipped-job-outputs-are-empty.md) — 2026-08-17 — Seven load-bearing facts for reviewing a digest-bound release pipeline, each traced to upstream README/docs source or verified locally against docker buildx v0.36.1 and Docker Hub. Three are footguns that pass casual review: (1) the most-copied `download-artifact` snippet is the `merge-multiple: true` FLATTENING example, so a promote job globbing `/tmp/x/*` under the default `false` gets directories not files; (2) `if: always() && ...` — the exact idiom for running promote after a partially-failed matrix — lets a skipped/failed job's output interpolate to `''` rather than erroring, and the official `needs` example payload shows a failed job with `"outputs": {}`, so an empty digest can reach a tagging step unless you gate on `result == 'success'` explicitly; (3) `imagetools inspect --format '{{json .Manifest.Digest}}'` returns a JSON-QUOTED 73-byte string (`"` + 71-char digest + `"`, final byte 0x22) with NO trailing newline, so comparing it against `build-push-action`'s unquoted `outputs.digest` never matches and a defensive newline-strip is a misleading no-op. Confirmed sound: `outputs.digest` resolves to buildkit's `containerimage.digest` (traced through `main.ts` -> actions-toolkit `resolveDigest`), includes the `sha256:` prefix, and for a multi-arch push IS the OCI index digest — byte-identical to the registry's own `docker-content-digest` header and to `.Manifest.Digest`, so digest-binding promotion is valid in principle. Also: `upload-artifact` roots at the least common ancestor so a single-file `path: <dir>/core` stores as `core` at the artifact root; a matrix job is ONE `needs` node whose `result` is `'failure'` if any leg failed under `fail-fast: false`, and its outputs are "combined from all jobs inside the matrix" (same-named outputs across legs are last-writer-wins, i.e. nondeterministic); and `imagetools create` is a registry-side operation that ran fine with only the built-in `default` docker driver, so a promote-only job does NOT need `setup-buildx-action`.

- [Protocol and standards sites cap page content well above 1080px (median 1312 at a 1920 viewport, a third uncapped) and keep heading-plus-sentence lists single-column even with room for two, reserving multi-column grids for link cards](standards-body-sites/protocol-and-standards-sites-cap-content-well-above-1080px-and-keep-prose-lists-single-column-reserving-multi-column-grids-for-link-cards.md) — 2026-08-14 — Measured live with Playwright at 1920 across 24 pages (W3C, IETF, MCP, Matrix, OpenAPI, OAuth.net, ActivityPub, OpenTelemetry, CNCF, Kubernetes, Rust, Deno, Astro, SQLite, Let's Encrypt, Node.js, Tailscale, Proton), not read from CSS. Only 2 of the 16 pages that cap at all cap at or below 1080 (OAuth.net 960, Deno 864); median 1312, and 8 of 24 are uncapped full-viewport. Prose measure is bounded separately and much narrower (482-976), so a wider shell buys margin and figure room, not line length. On grids the first-pass rule ("grids are only ever link cards") was WRONG and is corrected here: 17 of 24 multi-column blocks are link cards but 6 are genuine prose grids, including a Let's Encrypt block with 13 words/item and zero links. What survives is narrower — among standards/governance pages specifically, comparable heading-plus-sentence lists stay stacked even in blocks wide enough for two columns (IETF participate 4 items/17 words in 1300px, Kubernetes contribute 7 items/28 words in 1204px, OpenTelemetry 19 items in 1220px, W3C, Rust). Method caution recorded: absence of grids is weak evidence until you separately confirm the zero-grid sites HAVE the content and the room. Also: docs surfaces pin the rail to the viewport edge while the same site's marketing pages centre (Cloudflare logo x=24 vs x=224), so a masthead that shifts between the two surfaces is normal for the class. Third axis, the one most likely to be guessed wrong: FIGURES DO NOT OUTSET. Table width is a median 1.00x of prose (n=6, max 1.50x), code 1.00x (n=11, max 1.25x), images 0.76x (n=7, never once wider than prose); nothing anywhere exceeded 1.50x, and exactly-flush is modal at scale (GitHub 135 code blocks, PostgreSQL 37, Deno 10). The intuition that tables are data and deserve room is not what this class does. Drove the pdpp.dev concept-page container relief (1080 -> 1400 above 1600px), the decision NOT to two-column /participate and /self-host, and the figure bound (900 -> 720, tables from 1.50x to 1.20x).

- [Vana's DataPortabilityEscrow lets FACILITATOR_ROLE pay an arbitrary recipient from any account's in-escrow balance, so a destination allowlist cannot bound the facilitator key and a per-transaction value cap is mandatory — while the mainnet Safe can revoke the role but two Moksha deployments are still admin'd by the compromised PRO-807 key](validator-key-custody/vana-escrow-facilitator-role-can-pay-arbitrary-recipients-so-a-destination-allowlist-is-insufficient-and-a-value-cap-is-mandatory.md) — 2026-08-14 — Resolves the conditional in `kms-design.md` execution step 5 the expensive way: the implementation's NatSpec says `settle` "pays an arbitrary external `to`", and `_payout` checks only that the recipient is non-zero, so the proposed v1 `to`-allowlist cannot bound this key. Deeper point: an in-process allowlist or cap sits inside the very component the July-31 threat model assumes compromised, so enforcement must move to an external policy boundary — KMS closes key exfiltration but has no opinion about destinations or amounts. `settleBatch(SettleOp[])` elements each carry an independent recipient/amount, making element-wise policy decoding the concrete acceptance test for any candidate policy engine (a batch mixing one good and one bad op must fail). Good news on governance: mainnet Safe-A holds DEFAULT_ADMIN_ROLE and can revoke immediately; deploy-and-handoff cleanly renounced bootstrap privileges. Cleanup item with teeth: two Moksha escrow proxies still have the compromised PRO-807 key as DEFAULT_ADMIN_ROLE (verified twice), which makes Moksha a false-negative venue for rehearsing the revocation runbook. Method: Vana RPC caps `eth_getLogs` at 10k blocks; when the explorer 522s, selector extraction from bytecode + targeted `eth_call` works, but 4byte.directory returns troll collisions so names must be confirmed against the signature hash.

- [Outsider-clear PR/commit writing for a non-specialist-but-competent reader is real and independently verifiable, but in most beginner-friendly-reputation projects (Rails, Vue, Svelte, Astro, freeCodeCamp) it shows up as an exceptional outlier or as onboarding-culture tone, not as the house style — Django's ticket-format commits and Julia Evans's own PRs to tcpdump and git-scm.com are the two cases where it IS the typical/reputational style itself](writing-craft/outsider-clear-pr-writing-for-non-specialist-audiences-is-real-but-exceptional-not-typical-except-in-django-and-in-julia-evans-own-prs.md) — 2026-08-14 — Django commit `6e365f8` (CVE-2026-48588): plain-English bug explanation + credit line, and this is the norm across 19 sampled commits, not an exception. Julia Evans's own repo commits are ordinary/terse, but her PRs to outside projects (tcpdump#1413, git-scm.com#2040/#2053) carry the same "state my own confusion as motivating evidence" move that defines her blog writing — a real, verified transfer of craft from blog to PR prose, scoped to outsider-advocacy PRs specifically. freeCodeCamp is a clean negative: huge non-specialist contributor base and friendly onboarding culture, but sampled PR bodies are checklist-template-only with no causal "why" — onboarding tone is not writing craft.
